People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. For about the past year, even small things—a phone buzz, someone walking by my office, or just some random thought—can derail me before I really catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. If I’m supposed to sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the volunteer meetings at church, I get this feeling like I need to move. Like there’s a motor running inside, I guess. I’ll mess with my hands or look for something to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s more like… I’ll cut in before I think about it. I’ve jumped into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finished an employee’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it annoys people.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel like I’m following at first, but then a few minutes later I realize I missed a lot of what was said. I’ll have to ask them to go back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be on the go or run around, even when you're not supposed to be doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not running around exactly, but when I’m supposed to just sit, like at dinner or a church meeting, I feel like I need to move or do something with my hands. Like I can’t really just be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll cut people off without meaning to. Like with my wife’s conversations, or at work I’ll finish someone’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it doesn’t always land well.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt something out before they were even done asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty following rules or waiting for your turn as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. Teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much or get up without asking. I’d blurt out answers before they were even done with the question sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in class when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’d be listening at first, but after a few minutes I’d sort of realize I’d missed part of what the teacher was saying. I still do that now in meetings sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as overly energetic or hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I don’t know if people used that exact word, but looking back, as a kid teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for not following instructions as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I got in trouble some. Mostly teachers moving my seat because I was talking too much, getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answering before they finished asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and impulsivity your entire life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty much always been there. Looking back, it was definitely around when I was a kid—teachers moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted stuff out before they even finished.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it does cause friction. At work people can get impatient with me in meetings or when I’m supposed to be mentoring someone and it doesn’t go smoothly. And at home… it’s started to affect trust a bit when family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. For about the past year, even small things—a phone buzz, someone walking by my office, or just some random thought—can derail me before I really catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. If I’m supposed to sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the volunteer meetings at church, I get this feeling like I need to move. Like there’s a motor running inside, I guess. I’ll mess with my hands or look for something to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s more like… I’ll cut in before I think about it. I’ve jumped into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finished an employee’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it annoys people.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel like I’m following at first, but then a few minutes later I realize I missed a lot of what was said. I’ll have to ask them to go back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be on the go or run around, even when you're not supposed to be doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not running around exactly, but when I’m supposed to just sit, like at dinner or a church meeting, I feel like I need to move or do something with my hands. Like I can’t really just be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll cut people off without meaning to. Like with my wife’s conversations, or at work I’ll finish someone’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it doesn’t always land well.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt something out before they were even done asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty following rules or waiting for your turn as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. Teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much or get up without asking. I’d blurt out answers before they were even done with the question sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in class when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’d be listening at first, but after a few minutes I’d sort of realize I’d missed part of what the teacher was saying. I still do that now in meetings sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as overly energetic or hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I don’t know if people used that exact word, but looking back, as a kid teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for not following instructions as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I got in trouble some. Mostly teachers moving my seat because I was talking too much, getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answering before they finished asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and impulsivity your entire life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty much always been there. Looking back, it was definitely around when I was a kid—teachers moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted stuff out before they even finished.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it does cause friction. At work people can get impatient with me in meetings or when I’m supposed to be mentoring someone and it doesn’t go smoothly. And at home… it’s started to affect trust a bit when family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these symptoms were not a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s been around, but it’s been harder to manage for maybe… a little over a year now. Since the issues with my in-laws started taking up more of our family life, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I’m still keeping up overall. But it does bother me that people at work lose patience with me, and at home it’s… starting to affect trust when plans don’t go the way they should. I’ll mess with my hands or look for something to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. It’s more like… I’ll cut in before I think about it. I’ve jumped into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finished an employee’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it annoys people.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel like I’m following at first, but then a few minutes later I realize I missed a lot of what was said. I’ll have to ask them to go back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be on the go or run around, even when you're not supposed to be doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not running around exactly, but when I’m supposed to just sit, like at dinner or a church meeting, I feel like I need to move or do something with my hands. Like I can’t really just be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll cut people off without meaning to. Like with my wife’s conversations, or at work I’ll finish someone’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it doesn’t always land well.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt something out before they were even done asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty following rules or waiting for your turn as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. Teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much or get up without asking. I’d blurt out answers before they were even done with the question sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in class when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’d be listening at first, but after a few minutes I’d sort of realize I’d missed part of what the teacher was saying. I still do that now in meetings sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as overly energetic or hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I don’t know if people used that exact word, but looking back, as a kid teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for not following instructions as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I got in trouble some. Mostly teachers moving my seat because I was talking too much, getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answering before they finished asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and impulsivity your entire life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty much always been there. Looking back, it was definitely around when I was a kid—teachers moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted stuff out before they even finished.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it does cause friction. At work people can get impatient with me in meetings or when I’m supposed to be mentoring someone and it doesn’t go smoothly. And at home… it’s started to affect trust a bit when family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these symptoms were not a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s been around, but it’s been harder to manage for maybe… a little over a year now. Since the issues with my in-laws started taking up more of our family life, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I’m still keeping up overall. But it does bother me that people at work lose patience with me, and at home it’s… starting to affect trust when plans don’t go the way they should. So, yeah, some distress.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're sitting still as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or a church meeting, I get this… kind of motor-running feeling. I usually need to shift around or mess with something in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms ever caused you to lose a job, damage a significant relationship, or significantly impair your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that. I’m still functioning, mostly. But it has started to affect things… like people at work getting impatient when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go smoothly, and at home when family plans fall through, it costs me some trust.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Not running around exactly, but when I’m supposed to just sit, like at dinner or a church meeting, I feel like I need to move or do something with my hands. Like I can’t really just be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll cut people off without meaning to. Like with my wife’s conversations, or at work I’ll finish someone’s thought before they’re done. I can tell it doesn’t always land well.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt something out before they were even done asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty following rules or waiting for your turn as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. Teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much or get up without asking. I’d blurt out answers before they were even done with the question sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in class when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’d be listening at first, but after a few minutes I’d sort of realize I’d missed part of what the teacher was saying. I still do that now in meetings sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as overly energetic or hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I don’t know if people used that exact word, but looking back, as a kid teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for not following instructions as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I got in trouble some. Mostly teachers moving my seat because I was talking too much, getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answering before they finished asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and impulsivity your entire life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty much always been there. Looking back, it was definitely around when I was a kid—teachers moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted stuff out before they even finished.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it does cause friction. At work people can get impatient with me in meetings or when I’m supposed to be mentoring someone and it doesn’t go smoothly. And at home… it’s started to affect trust a bit when family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these symptoms were not a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s been around, but it’s been harder to manage for maybe… a little over a year now. Since the issues with my in-laws started taking up more of our family life, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I’m still keeping up overall. But it does bother me that people at work lose patience with me, and at home it’s… starting to affect trust when plans don’t go the way they should. So, yeah, some distress.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're sitting still as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or a church meeting, I get this… kind of motor-running feeling. I usually need to shift around or mess with something in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms ever caused you to lose a job, damage a significant relationship, or significantly impair your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that. I’m still functioning, mostly. But it has started to affect things… like people at work getting impatient when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go smoothly, and at home when family plans fall through, it costs me some trust.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like these symptoms have significantly held you back from achieving your goals or reaching your full potential at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, so I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. But it does feel like it’s starting to cost me… people’s patience at work, and some trust at home when plans or conversations don’t go how they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few months where you didn't experience any of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s been harder to manage for… about a year and a couple months now, since the in-law conflict started taking over more of our family life. Before that I guess it was there, but not as noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’d blurt out answers before they were even done with the question sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in class when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’d be listening at first, but after a few minutes I’d sort of realize I’d missed part of what the teacher was saying. I still do that now in meetings sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as overly energetic or hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I don’t know if people used that exact word, but looking back, as a kid teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, get up without asking, or blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for not following instructions as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I got in trouble some. Mostly teachers moving my seat because I was talking too much, getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answering before they finished asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and impulsivity your entire life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty much always been there. Looking back, it was definitely around when I was a kid—teachers moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted stuff out before they even finished.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it does cause friction. At work people can get impatient with me in meetings or when I’m supposed to be mentoring someone and it doesn’t go smoothly. It’s been around, but it’s been harder to manage for maybe… a little over a year now. Since the issues with my in-laws started taking up more of our family life, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I’m still keeping up overall. But it does bother me that people at work lose patience with me, and at home it’s… starting to affect trust when plans don’t go the way they should. So, yeah, some distress.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're sitting still as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or a church meeting, I get this… kind of motor-running feeling. I usually need to shift around or mess with something in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms ever caused you to lose a job, damage a significant relationship, or significantly impair your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that. I’m still functioning, mostly. But it has started to affect things… like people at work getting impatient when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go smoothly, and at home when family plans fall through, it costs me some trust.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like these symptoms have significantly held you back from achieving your goals or reaching your full potential at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, so I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. But it does feel like it’s starting to cost me… people’s patience at work, and some trust at home when plans or conversations don’t go how they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few months where you didn't experience any of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.
